## quality-method

**Question.** Does AIW support the quality attribute method that Quality Architecture Vol 9, Architecture Assurance and Applied Solution Architecture Vol 10 prescribe (taxonomy, six-part scenarios, utility tree and prioritisation, tactics, sensitivity and trade-off points, risks, evaluation, fitness functions and continuous verification, budgets/SLOs, and flow of targets to components, technology, deployment and tests)? AIW paths are relative to /home/user/AIW-Brain/AIW-V5-Local-Source.

**Answer.** AIW covers the first half of the method well. It has a fixed taxonomy of 18 quality concerns in 5 families from the SA Playbook, a structured quality scenario with a target that must be measurable before anyone can confirm it, a utility tree built from the records that shows uncovered attributes as holes, recorded pairwise conflicts, and a decision reading that runs without an LLM. That reading rules out alternatives that break a hard constraint, finds dominated alternatives, tests whether the lead survives other weightings and flags priority-sensitivity points; What-if and the scale readings then turn targets into availability, recovery and latency budget checks along the chain from driver to plan. The back half is only partly there, mostly as derived readings rather than records a person keeps: the scenario has no source-of-stimulus or artefact field and no measurement contract, priority is a single High/Medium/Low value, trade-offs are pairs, sensitivity and trade-off points and risks are observations nobody can save, Chapter 11 has no walkthrough of each high-priority scenario, and budgets are calculated but never allocated to owners. Several things AIW cannot do at all today: make a quality scenario (as opposed to a constraint driver) a mandatory feasibility gate, record importance against difficulty or evidence maturity, record an SLO or error budget (or say whether an objective is event-based or time-based), or tie a driver to a repeated verification check (a fitness function) with review triggers. In live runs (port 4637, scratch database), bank-payment had 6 of 6 drivers measurable but 0 of 6 carried by a plan. In clinic-appointments, the journey drafter had turned the 6 functional requirements into quality drivers: 5 defaulted to 'integrity' and none was measurable. Sol stays advisory throughout: field drafts never fill measure fields, and the trade-off pack and the catalogue's fitness tests reach Sol only as packet text.

**Skeptic on the answer.** Five corrections, plus one finding to add. (1) 'Trade-offs are pairs' and 'sensitivity and trade-off points and risks are observations nobody can save' are overstated. QC records are pairwise, but each Chapter 3 alternative saves a person-recorded effect on every driver its decision names, with reason and evidence (public/decisions-domain.js:193-196; public/record-fields.js:131). Chapter 9 security risks are records with owner and next review (public/record-fields.js:185). Only the sensitivity points and the quality 'risk' insights are derived. (2) 'Budgets are calculated but never allocated to owners' is wrong as stated. Chapter 10 and Chapter 7 targets name a driver with operator, value, unit, window, basis and confirmed (public/driver-links.js:6-12, :42-52), on plans with an on-call owner (public/record-fields.js:192). Chapter 6 capabilities hold a driver-linked recovery target with recoveryOwner (public/record-fields.js:148). What is missing is journey-segment budgets with combination rules, and SLO or error-budget records. (3) 'The trade-off pack… reach[es] Sol only as packet text' contradicts the analyst's own last gap: the pack's LN figures drive the deterministic SCALE-LATENCY-BUDGET Chapter 10 finding (public/desk-capacity.js:376-378, :525). (4) 'Field drafts never fill measure fields' holds only for metric, unit and window. Sol may still draft the verification method ('measurement') and the stimulus, conditions, response and tactic (public/field-suggestions.js:102-105). (5) Of the 18 concerns, 6 are explicitly not from the SA Playbook (public/quality-domain.js:26-36). Holes appear only for the 6 Playbook attributes with a guide (availability, performance, scalability, maintainability, deployability, usability); integrity, security, recoverability, traceability, testability and interoperability never show as holes (public/utility-model.js:44). Add from the live check: in clinic-appointments the ARS gate reads '0 of 6 measurable · 6 verified', because the drafted measurement text counts as a verification (public/ars-document.js:35, :166). The SDD already prints the decision trade-off reading with sensitivity points, and per-driver component mechanisms (public/sdd-architecture.js:689, :764-800, :1160-1164). The live figures quoted (bank 6/6 measurable, 0 carried; clinic 5 integrity, 0 measurable; potential trade-offs 5 and 0) match my own Node run of createProject.

**What AIW does today.**

- Taxonomy (exists, fixed): 18 quality concerns, including 6 added ones (compliance, privacy, cost, capacity, reliability, operability) with stimulus and metric guidance, at public/quality-domain.js:12-37. The 5 Playbook families are at public/playbook-knowledge.js:6. Added categories 'take part in no score, hard constraint or conformance check' (public/quality-domain.js:26-29).
- Chapter 1 non-functional requirements (partial): the only requirement type is 'Functional requirement' (public/requirements-domain.js:18-29). A quality word with no target is flagged as an ambiguity (public/statement-checks.js:12-13; public/requirements-domain.js:101-102), and acceptance criteria with timing claims need a boundary and threshold (public/requirements-domain.js:98-99).
- Scenario record (partial): quality.driver holds stimulus, conditions (the environment), response, metric, operator, targetValue, unit, window, measurement and targetConfirmed, plus links to requirements, responsibilities and components (public/record-fields.js:126; public/quality-domain.js:96, :225). There is no field for the source of the stimulus or the artefact.
- Scenario checks: 'scenario' requires stimulus, conditions and response (public/quality-domain.js:176). 'measurement' requires metric, operator, a finite value, unit, window and verification, with no placeholder or vague word (public/quality-domain.js:111-120, :177). A target cannot be confirmed until it is measurable (public/quality-domain.js:263). A driver must link at least one requirement and one responsibility (public/quality-domain.js:178, :180).
- Drafting by rule (Chapter 2): the journey drafter writes one quality scenario per requirement and defaults the category to the first concern, integrity, when the requirement contains no quality cue word (public/journey-drafter.js:271-279; QUALITY_TYPES[0] at public/quality-domain.js:13). Seen live on clinic-appointments: QD-001 to QD-006 mirror REQ-001 to REQ-006, 5 are 'integrity', 0 are measurable.
- Utility tree (exists, derived): the Model view runs utility, family, attribute, driver (public/instrument-tray.js:47-50; public/utility-model.js:1-55). A core Playbook attribute with no driver shows as a hole (public/utility-model.js:44, :151-152). Order follows priority, then rank (public/utility-model.js:32).
- Prioritisation (one-dimensional): High, Medium or Low plus an order within the group, and a rationale is required (public/quality-domain.js:77, :182, :276-277; public/quality.js:120).
- Trade-offs: QC conflict records join exactly two drivers and carry an owner and resolution; a later change to either driver reopens the record (public/quality-domain.js:139-140, :194, :278-281). potentialTradeoffs has 5 hard-coded category pairs and fires only when two drivers share a responsibility (public/quality-domain.js:141-146). Playbook attribute trade-offs appear as utility insights (public/design-reasoning.js:273-280; public/utility-model.js:170-176).
- Tactics: the Playbook holds 56 tactics. By attribute: availability 15, maintainability 13, scalability 7, performance 6, deployability 6, security 3, testability 3, usability 3; none for integrity, traceability or interoperability (counted from public/playbook-knowledge.js:7). driver.tactic is one free-text 'Candidate tactic — for evaluation' (public/record-fields.js:126). Whether the design 'names' a tactic is inferred from regex cues in chain text and runtime facts (public/design-reasoning.js:217-246). Released tactic claims are listed with their release id and reviewer (public/quality.js:129-131).
- Decision reading (exists, deterministic): an alternative is ruled out only when it breaks a hard constraint driver (public/decision-reading.js:10-12, :145-151). Default weights are High 3, Medium 2, Low 1, with effects of +1/0/-1 (public/decision-reading.js:35-40). It reports dominance and how firmly the lead holds across all weightings that keep the priority order (public/decision-reading.js:13-24). An unstated effect on a hard constraint is reported as 'not stated', not as a pass (public/decision-reading.js:178, :236). Its note says 'not a measurement; it does not choose' (public/decision-reading.js:44).
- Sensitivity points: AIW defines one as a driver whose one-step priority change flips a decision's lead (public/utility-model.js:129-142, :166-168; public/tradeoff-model.js:213-215). Insights of kind 'risk' (an unmet plan, an unconfirmed High target, a chosen option ruled out or dominated) are derived and never saved (public/utility-model.js:157, :161; public/tradeoff-model.js:196-206).
- Budgets in What-if: an availability percentage over a window becomes minutes of unavailability (public/design-reasoning.js:267-270, :318-327). Recovery targets are compared with plan and capability recovery minutes (public/design-reasoning.js:295-299, :328-336). A latency target is split equally across synchronous hops and the recorded timeouts are summed (public/design-reasoning.js:301-306, :339-347). Live on bank QD-003: '3 synchronous calls ... leave about 250 ms for each step'.
- Scale readings: demand per component is derived from rate, user, volume and latency targets; latency budget use is estimated from reference round-trip figures; SCALE-NO-TARGET, UNDER-PROVISIONED, LATENCY-BUDGET and UNCONFIRMED join the Chapter 10 checks as items to confirm (public/desk-capacity.js:366-375, :396-410, :430-531).
- Flow of targets: one chain runs from each driver to requirements, responsibilities, components, capabilities, products, plans, decisions and contracts (public/design-reasoning.js:130-156). Chapter 6, 7 and 10 target rows of kind availability, recovery time, data loss, latency, throughput or capacity name a driver and are compared with its target (public/driver-links.js:1-49). The ARS gate checks every driver is measurable, names a verification and is carried by a plan, capability or realisation, and lists disagreements (public/ars-document.js:163-183; public/readiness-model.js:223-229). It informs and blocks nothing.
- Quantitative evidence: guided design tasks offer analytical models (backlog drain, read cost, restore budget, retry budget, replica capacity, transport budget), each with stated assumptions (public/quality-analysis.js:4-12). Measured samples are compared with the model's prediction, using mean and standard deviation (public/quality-analysis.js:91-111), and a stale measurement raises a Chapter 11 warning (public/quality-analysis.js:116).
- Verification: a typed check (metric or assertion, operator, expected value, unit, environment, release, procedure) is bound to a delivery action and an accepted obligation (public/architecture-verification.js:11-27). 'Verified' requires a passing comparison and matching evidence digests (public/review-domain.js:395-403).
- Chapter 11 evaluation: architecture cases have 6 observation dimensions, including 'Alternatives and trade-offs' (public/architecture-case.js:19). End-to-end mode checks 9 conditions along one requirement spine, including a confirmed quality target, two compared approaches and a current recorded test (public/architecture-case.js:151-164).
- Sol (proposes only): 'Compare products' over saved options and quality scenarios is 'a question, not a score' (public/sol-comparison.js:1-2). Catalogue fitness tests reach Sol only as 'verificationHints' in packet text (public/knowledge-structure.js:33, :128-131). Field drafts never fill measure fields (public/record-fields.js:54-56; public/field-suggestions.js:102).
- Tests: utility-validate.mjs passed 8 checks, tradeoff-validate.mjs passed 9 and scale-readings-validate.mjs passed 6 (run read-only). Example assertions: availabilityBudget('99.9',30) equals 43.2 (utility-validate.mjs:59); 'the playbook has no integrity tactics, and none are invented' (utility-validate.mjs:55). scale-validate.mjs and scale-browser-validate.mjs test AIW's own performance at 500 records, not the user's quality method (scale-validate.mjs:1-20).

**Gaps.**

- **[high | 2 Drivers / 3 Decisions | effort M] A quality scenario cannot be a mandatory feasibility gate; breaking it is weighed as tension and can be outweighed**
  - AIW: 'Breaks it' is refused for any driver that is not a constraint (public/decisions-domain.js:193-195). Only constraint drivers are hard (public/decision-reading.js:145-151). So bank QD-001 'At most 0 extra debits' (High) counts as -3 at most and other drivers can outweigh it (public/decision-reading.js:35-40). The only workaround is a separate constraint driver linked to a Chapter 1 constraint.
  - Handbook: Quality_Architecture_Volume_9_Handbook › QAE-FND-05 Treat constraints, objectives and preferences differently: duplicates 'cannot earn its way back into consideration through a weighted average'. Same handbook › QAE-OS-003 Hard constraints and negotiable objectives › How the mechanism works (pass, fail or unknown per predicate). Architecture_Assurance_Production_Readiness_and_Evolution › 11 Architecture evolution › Eligibility comes before preference scoring. Applied_Solution_Architecture_Volume_10_Handbook › Compare feasible designs and record the decision.
  - Recommendation: Add a 'mandatory' flag to a quality driver, set only by a person (with the authorship guard) and requiring an owner and an authority source. The decision reading would then treat the target as a pass/fail/unknown predicate. Unknown is never eligible as the working choice, and the SDD shows the feasibility table before any weighted total. Knowledge and Sol may suggest the flag but never set it.
  - Skeptic: HOLDS — Confirmed. 'Breaks it' is refused for any driver that is not a constraint (public/decisions-domain.js:193-195). Only constraint drivers can be hard (public/decision-reading.js:145-151, :168). A constraint driver also carries no measurable target, because only quality drivers are measured (public/quality-domain.js:62-66, :162-175), so the workaround loses the target. The gap is slightly worse than stated: even for a hard constraint, an unknown effect does not exclude the alternative. Eligibility removes only alternatives that break a constraint (public/decision-reading.js:178-180), and the unknown counts as 0 at the default weights (:61). That contradicts Architecture_Assurance_Production_Readiness_and_Evolution › 11 Architecture evolution › Eligibility comes before preference scoring ('Fail and unknown are not eligible for selection'). The citations to Quality_Architecture_Volume_9_Handbook › QAE-FND-05 ('cannot earn its way back… through a weighted average') and to Applied_Solution_Architecture_Volume_10_Handbook › Compare feasible designs and record the decision ('Evaluate mandatory conditions first') are accurate.
- **[high | 2 Drivers | effort M] The scenario has no source or artefact field and no measurement contract (population, statistic, cutoff, unknowns)**
  - AIW: The fields are stimulus, conditions, response, metric, operator, value, unit, window and measurement only (public/record-fields.js:126). The artefact is approximated by responsibilityIds and componentIds, and the source sits inside the stimulus text. Measurability is checked on the text: a seven-word vague list plus field presence (public/quality-domain.js:111-120), so a metric reading just 'latency' passes. The percentile has to live in the metric text (public/quality-domain.js:127).
  - Handbook: Quality_Architecture_Volume_9_Handbook › QAE-FND-02 Turn an objective into a testable quality scenario (six elements). Same handbook › QAE-OS-002 Quality attribute scenarios and measurable promises › How the mechanism works and › Outcome to mechanism decision map › Measurement contract; › QAE-TPL-01 Quality scenario card; › QAE-TPL-02 Measurement contract. Applied_Solution_Architecture_Volume_10_Handbook › Requirement and measurement contract (ASA-TPL-01). Architecture_Assurance_Production_Readiness_and_Evolution › 3 Qualification experiment design › Define workload populations before computing performance.
  - Recommendation: Add a stimulus source (linked to a Chapter 1 actor, stakeholder or external party) and a typed artefact link. Add a versioned measurement-contract sub-record: unit and identity, eligible population, numerator and denominator or a named statistic and percentile, window, evidence cutoff, treatment of unknowns, instrumentation owner. Require it before targetConfirmed. Keep these as measure fields that Sol never drafts.
  - Skeptic: HOLDS — Confirmed (public/record-fields.js:126; public/quality-domain.js:96, :111-120, :225). Nuances: componentIds is a typed link to Chapter 5 components and partly stands in for the artefact (public/quality-domain.js:225). Population is only prompted, as free text inside the window field ('Define the workload, population and measurement window', public/journey-drafter.js:286); no structured population, numerator or denominator, cutoff or unknown treatment exists. The handbook citations check out: Quality_Architecture_Volume_9_Handbook › QAE-FND-02 (six elements) and › QAE-FND-04 Measure a stable population and preserve unknown outcomes (a metric contract with eligibility, numerator and denominator, cutoff and missing data). Applied_Solution_Architecture_Volume_10_Handbook › Requirement and measurement contract (ASA-TPL-01) has Scenario and Population rows.
- **[high | 1 Requirements / 2 Drivers | effort M] Functional requirements are drafted as quality scenarios, defaulting to 'integrity'; Chapter 1 has no non-functional requirement type**
  - AIW: Chapter 1 has only a 'Functional requirement' type (public/requirements-domain.js:18-29), and every quality driver must link one (public/quality-domain.js:178). The journey drafter writes 'one quality scenario per requirement without one' and falls back to QUALITY_TYPES[0], integrity (public/journey-drafter.js:271-279; public/quality-domain.js:13). Live clinic-appointments run: 'Quality: Book an appointment at any time…' and four more were drafted as integrity, all unmeasurable, which put 5 false integrity leaves in the utility tree and left 4 decisions unweighed.
  - Handbook: Applied_Solution_Architecture_Volume_10_Handbook › Translate business promises into measurable requirements: 'Write functional obligations, quality scenarios, constraints and assumptions separately'. Quality_Architecture_Volume_9_Handbook › QAE-CMP-01 Outcome, quality attribute, measure and mechanism. Same handbook › QAE-OS-005 Stakeholder priorities and utility trees › Technical depth › State trace and invariants: 'A quality label alone is not a leaf'.
  - Recommendation: Draft a quality scenario only when a requirement or brief passage carries a quality cue or a figure. Otherwise propose nothing, or leave the category as an open question rather than defaulting to integrity. Add a Chapter 1 quality-requirement (NFR) artefact, or let a driver anchor on an outcome, constraint or source passage, so a quality need does not have to borrow a functional requirement.
  - Skeptic: HOLDS — Confirmed by my own run: createProject for clinic-appointments, called in Node with a scratch path. QD-001 to QD-004 and QD-006 are 'integrity' and QD-005 is 'performance'. None is measurable. Every driver has priority '', and the project has 4 decisions. In the drafter the category is a basis-'gap' value carrying the note 'No quality cue word… choose the quality' (public/journey-drafter.js:279). The reference journey commits it unchanged (shared/reference-journeys.js:7-15), and the saved driver flags nothing as open on category: category is not in DRIVER_FIELDS (public/quality-domain.js:96), and d.open is undefined in the run. No Chapter 1 type or requirement-group kind is non-functional (public/requirements-domain.js:18-29; public/requirement-groups.js:31). A quality driver with only requirement-group or outcome links still gets the 'requirements' error (public/quality-domain.js:178).
- **[high | 2 Drivers / 8 Interfaces / 10 Deployment | effort L] No SLO, SLI or error-budget record; budgets are calculated readings, not owned allocations; percentile targets are split as if they add**
  - AIW: availabilityBudget always converts a percentage to minutes (public/design-reasoning.js:267-270); nothing records whether an objective is event-based or time-based. A latency target is divided equally per synchronous hop (public/design-reasoning.js:342), even when the metric is a '95th percentile' (bank QD-003, public/quality-domain.js:127). There is no percentile caveat (grep found none in design-reasoning, utility-model or desk-capacity). Chapter 8 caller budgets (public/architecture-applicability.js:167-168) are not linked to any driver. Plan targets are a kind plus a value (public/driver-links.js:22-23). scaleTarget read clinic QD-005's 15-minute workflow deadline as a 900,000 ms request latency (public/desk-capacity.js:396-410; live run).
  - Handbook: Applied_Solution_Architecture_Volume_10_Handbook › Use quantitative models within their assumptions: 99.9% 'is not automatically equivalent to 43.2 minutes… Define whether the objective is event-based or time-based'. Quality_Architecture_Volume_9_Handbook › QAE-OS-004 Quality budgets across a business journey › How the mechanism works (assign a local owner to each segment) and › When to select it ('Avoid when percentiles are being added'). Same handbook › QAE-PC-001 Latency budgets and tail behaviour › How the mechanism works. Architecture_Assurance_Production_Readiness_and_Evolution › 10 Observability and operations › Fix the population before counting success (burn rate). Applied_Solution_Architecture_Volume_10_Handbook › Chapter 41 Service levels unit economics and fleet operations › How the mechanism works.
  - Recommendation: Add an SLO record linked to a driver and to plans: SLI specification with eligible, good and bad events and exclusions; event-based or time-based objective; window; error budget; burn-rate alert policy; owner. Add person-entered budget allocations per journey segment, each with owner and combination rule (serial upper bound versus percentile versus critical path). Mark the equal split as an upper-bound sketch and suppress it when the metric is a percentile. Separate request latency from journey deadlines in scaleTarget.
  - Skeptic: HOLDS — The core holds. There is no SLO, SLI, error-budget or burn-rate record; the catalogue has PAT-SLO-BASED-OPERATIONS and PAT-ERROR-BUDGET only as knowledge patterns (public/brain-catalogue.js:107, :251). The equal per-hop split (public/design-reasoning.js:342) and the absence of any percentile caveat are both confirmed. Two parts are overstated. (1) 'Plan targets are a kind plus a value' is wrong: each Chapter 10 or Chapter 7 target carries kind, driverId, operator, value, unit, window, basis and confirmed (public/driver-links.js:6-12, :42-52). Plans have a 'Service / on-call owner' (public/record-fields.js:192), and a Chapter 6 capability holds a recovery target with recoveryOwner and driverId (public/record-fields.js:148). So person-entered, owned allocations per part do exist. What is missing is a journey-segment budget with a combination rule, plus SLI and error-budget semantics. (2) The scaleTarget misreading reaches beyond clinic QD-005: bank QD-004, a 15-minute investigation deadline, also parses as 900,000 ms latency, because its text contains 'response' (public/desk-capacity.js:382, :408). It is not used there only because QD-003 comes first (:438-439).

  - AIW: Typed checks belong to a delivery action and an accepted obligation and are compared once per release (public/architecture-verification.js:11-27; public/review-domain.js:395-403); nothing compares their threshold with the Chapter 2 target. Quality measurements are compared with the model's prediction, not the driver target, using the mean only (public/quality-analysis.js:108-110). The ARS counts a driver as 'verified' when its measurement text is non-empty (public/ars-document.js:35). Fitness tests from the catalogue reach only Sol packets (public/knowledge-structure.js:128). There is no review or expiry trigger on drivers or decisions.

Same handbook › QAE-FND-10 Make a bounded decision and keep it reviewable; › Cross-volume continuity (EVO-VA-001 Architectural fitness functions → QAE-DP-005). Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › EVO-VA-001 Architectural fitness functions. Applied_Solution_Architecture_Volume_10_Handbook › Architecture experiment and evidence record (ASA-TPL-05). Architecture_Assurance_Production_Readiness_and_Evolution › 3 Qualification experiment design › Make the conclusion transferable and revocable.

It goes stale when the target changes. Add an observable 'review trigger' with an owner to drivers and decisions. Observations stay evidence that a person reviews; they never auto-pass, and productionAccepted remains false.

They are compared with the model's prediction, and the standard deviation is stored too (:108-109). (b) A Chapter 11 delivery action can link a quality driver as its object, with owner, due date, acceptance criteria and a one-off Verified status (public/review-domain.js:123, :306-311). Its 'assurance' template asks for 'a repeatable check' (:330). There is still no cadence, no threshold derived from the driver target, and no review trigger on drivers or decisions; decision.save has no trigger field (public/record-fields.js:130). The only next-review date is on Chapter 9 security risks (public/record-fields.js:185). The live clinic run strengthens the ARS point: the gate reads '0 of 6 measurable · 6 verified', because the drafted measurement text 'Verify against the acceptance criteria of REQ-001, once they say what is observed' counts as verified (public/ars-document.js:35, :166).

Quality_Architecture_Volume_9_Handbook › Primary-source register › SRC-QA-R-009.

  - AIW: Priority is High, Medium or Low plus a rank (public/quality-domain.js:77, :276-277). The driver has no stakeholderIds, difficulty, risk or evidence-maturity field (public/record-fields.js:126), and the utility tree orders by priority only (public/utility-model.js:32).

  - AIW: A conflict joins exactly two drivers (public/quality-domain.js:279-280). Potential trade-offs come from 5 hard-coded pairs and only when responsibilities are shared (public/quality-domain.js:141-146): bank-payment got 5, clinic-appointments got 0. A sensitivity point is a flip caused by a priority change (public/utility-model.js:129-142). Same handbook › QAE-OS-002 › Technical depth › Parameters and consequences; › QAE-CMP-04 (sensitivity: 'What change would reverse the decision?').

Add a sensitivity/trade-off point record attached to a decision or to a Chapter 5, 8 or 10 parameter, naming the drivers it moves in opposite directions and the 'wrong choice' consequence. The reading names where the leader and the runner-up pull apart (public/decision-reading.js:243), and the Model flags a working choice in tension with a High driver (public/tradeoff-model.js:204). The recommendation's deterministic 'support one, tension another' proposal therefore largely restates that existing reading. The real missing piece is a parameter-level sensitivity or trade-off point record (timeouts, replicas, TTLs) and a non-pairwise QC record.

Half of the recommendation is already built: the SCALE-LATENCY-BUDGET finding already names the figure ids and says 'Reference figures for estimation (…), not measurements' (public/desk-capacity.js:525). The pack's authority text is quoted correctly (knowledge/brain-tradeoffs.json 'authority').

## data-and-integration

**Question.** Does AIW support DATA ARCHITECTURE and INTEGRATION ARCHITECTURE as the Data_Architecture_Handbook and the Integration_Architecture_Volume_7_Handbook prescribe? This covers Chapter 8 (Interfaces & data), its links to Chapters 4, 5, 9 and 10, its checks, its Model views and its SDD output.

**Answer.** Only partly. Integration contracts are well supported. Chapter 8 records request, event and batch contracts between Chapter 5 components, Chapter 7 realizations and external parties. It checks correlation and idempotency keys against the payload, and requires failure, retry and timeout policies. A rule-assessed execution agreement covers delivery, repeat protection and authoritative outcomes. AIW also imports and exports OpenAPI 3.1 and AsyncAPI 3.0, previews the impact of a change across Chapters 1 to 10, and feeds Chapter 9 threats and Chapter 10 runtime paths. Data architecture is thinner: a one-level data dictionary with an owner, an authoritative system, six classification levels, a personal-data marker, residency and retention text, lifecycles, invariants and lineage from one definition to another. Versioning, error handling, partner integration, contract testing, retention, lineage, data quality and transaction boundaries are supported only partly, mostly as prose that is checked for presence. AIW cannot express ordering scope, dead-letter ownership, orchestration versus choreography or sagas, CDC, streaming or ETL mechanisms, master or reference data, conceptual, logical and physical model levels, or steward and custodian roles, and Chapters 9 and 10 never enforce residency or purpose. Catalogue patterns for these reach Chapter 8 only through Sol. The Chapter 5 and Chapter 8 ownership records can disagree without any finding: I checked this on a bank-payment project created through POST /api/projects (port 4637, scratch database under /home/user/hb-review/scratch/data-integration/, server stopped by PID). Paths are relative to /home/user/AIW-Brain/AIW-V5-Local-Source.

**Skeptic on the answer.** Five points in the direct answer need correcting. (1) 'AIW cannot express ordering scope, dead-letter ownership' is overstated. Ordering scope (none / per-key / global) and poison-work recovery with an owner are captured and rule-checked in the queued interaction and Publication design tasks (public/architecture-knowledge.js:101,109,151; public/composition-knowledge.js:38-39). They travel as accepted obligations to the SDD (public/sdd-architecture.js:894) and as prose into Chapter 5 interactions and seeded contract failurePolicy (public/architecture-design.js:159; public/interfaces-domain.js:107). So both are Partial, not missing; only the structured per-contract fields are missing. (2) 'Catalogue patterns for these reach Chapter 8 only through Sol' is misleading. AKR entries cannot be linked to Chapter 8 records, but outbox, delivery and recovery-copy patterns reach Chapter 8 through rule-based design tasks without Sol (public/composition-design.js:46-63; public/persistence-design.js:40-46). Released AKR concerns reach Chapter 3 through a governed task (public/architecture-design.js:90-92). (3) The Chapter 5 vs Chapter 8 ownership disagreement is real: no finding, and two conflicting 'owns' edges in the canonical graph (public/architecture-model.js:175,199). But the SDD follows the Chapter 8 authority (public/design-spec.js:152-158). After the change it printed APP-002 as owner, not APP-001; the analyst's SDD quote came from the unchanged project. (4) The 'six classification levels' are four sensitivity levels plus two open or unclassified states (public/interfaces-domain.js:21-22). (5) Freshness is not wholly absent: read copies and result reads declare freshness and a maximum age (public/read-design-knowledge.js:23,46; public/composition-manifest.js:75-76). The rest of the answer holds: a strong integration contract layer and a thinner data-architecture layer. My probes ran on bank-payment, port 4783, scratch DB /home/user/hb-review/scratch/skeptic-data/, server stopped by PID 3158.

**What AIW does today.**

- Contract record (IF-nnn): title, purpose, owner, contractVersion, protocol, operation, correlation and idempotency keys, duplicate, failure, timeout and retry policies, authorization, transport, compatibility, evidence and batch fields. A save is refused unless it names two different existing participants, and an event may not have a response payload: public/interfaces-domain.js:166-186, public/record-fields.js:165-168
- Participants are Chapter 5 components, Chapter 7 realizations and Chapter 8 parties: public/interfaces-domain.js:63
- Proposed contracts are seeded from Chapter 5 sync and event interactions, from Chapter 5 data reads of data another system owns, and from Chapter 7 connections. A retired proposal is never proposed again: public/interfaces-domain.js:103-107,127-133
- Contract checks: endpoints, definition, source change, payload, correlation key in request fields, duplicate (key in payload plus a duplicate policy), protection, failure/retry/timeout, timeout basis, compatibility, contract-review, schema-conflict, duplicate-contract, sample check, technology-choice, and batch schedule/restart/volume/order: public/interfaces-domain.js:289-318
- Execution agreement with 30 declared fields: delivery required or best-effort, publication atomic-commit/after-commit/direct, repetition, identity, protection, repeat owner, changed intent, meaning of a missing response, completion, outcome discovery, result contract, read freshness, caller retries, attempts, budgets and circuit breaker: public/composition-manifest.js:54-90
- Execution agreement assessed by rule against inspected references (AWS outbox, AWS idempotent APIs, MS async request-reply, MS transient faults, MS circuit breaker), each labelled a bounded interpretation: retained-intent, repeat-protection, stable-repeat-key, uncertain-result and outcome-discovery blockers at public/architecture-applicability.js:73-95; sources at public/composition-manifest.js:38-52; linked execution journeys of up to 12 contracts at public/interfaces-domain.js:319-324
- Data definition (DAT-nnn): owner, authoritative system plus up to 20 authorities with roles captures/posts/owns, classification (Not classified yet / Unclassified / Public / Internal / Confidential / Restricted), personal data yes/no, residency text, protection, retention policy, days, basis and confirmation, lifecycle and fields: public/interfaces-domain.js:21-25,43,187-203
- Field dictionary: typed fields (reference with cardinality, money with currency, date, date-time), key, required, enumeration, constraints, field classification and personal-data marker: public/interfaces-domain.js:24,204-224
- Data checks: data-owner (purpose, owner, authority), data-accountable-owner, classification, field-classification (a field more sensitive than its definition), retention, retention-assumption, key, field meaning, reference and currency, personal-data consistency, unconnected-data, data-review, lifecycle links: public/interfaces-domain.js:326-345
- Lifecycle state machine per definition, with transitions moved by a contract or by a schedule: public/interfaces-domain.js:51-55,146-156
- Invariants (balance, uniqueness, ordering, completeness) bound to data and contracts, with a check and an owner. A posting contract must keep an invariant or name an idempotency key: public/interfaces-domain.js:29-39,233-238,346-347
- Batch chain ordered by predecessor contracts, with cycle detection and Chapter 10 batch windows: public/interfaces-domain.js:239-248,325
- Lineage from one data definition to another or to a party, optionally through a contract, with transformation and owner checked: public/interfaces-domain.js:249-257,348. Field-level lineage between JSON-schema definitions: public/standards-domain.js:53-55
- Parties with kind, status (current/target/retiring) and integration fields. When those fields are empty they are derived from decided contracts, then from proposals labelled as such: public/interfaces-domain.js:40-41,225-231,258-276
- Design-time sample check: a synthetic payload is checked against the flat dictionary under normal, duplicate and timeout scenarios. It states that nested structures, security enforcement and processing behaviour need implementation tests: public/interfaces-domain.js:368-376. The check is recorded only after all four stages are visited: :398
- Contract or data review needs a named reviewer, evidence and a confirmation. The handoff to Security needs a current review and an acknowledgement: public/interfaces-domain.js:397,400
- OpenAPI 3.1 and AsyncAPI 3.0 import of 1 to 40 operations after a reviewed preview, a bounded JSON Schema subset validator, and OpenAPI, AsyncAPI and schema export that lists x-aiw-export-gaps: public/standards-domain.js:10-23,25-57,60-93. On bank-payment the OpenAPI export held one path (/payments) and 5 export gaps
- Impact preview: a contract or data change is listed against linked Chapter 1-10 records (responsibilities, components, controls, runtime plans), with signals for removed, renamed, retyped or newly required fields and narrowed values. Applying it needs a reviewed, current preview: public/interface-impact.js:30-68
- Guided exchange design task in four rule-based steps, which refuses an unclassified new definition or keys missing from the dictionary: public/exchange-design.js:8-13,30,37
- Model: Data flow view with the system of record starred (public/exchange-view.js:292,348). Pictures for Integration, Data & authority, Data lineage, Application cooperation, Sequence and Data lifecycle: public/notation-model.js:49-60,67. Insights for long synchronous waits, external requests without a timeout, retries without repeat recognition, contracts with no authority, and confidential data leaving to a party: public/exchange-model.js:303-332
- Anti-patterns detected by rule in Chapter 11: ANTI-SYNCHRONOUS-CHAIN, ANTI-MISSING-IDEMPOTENCY, ANTI-RETRY-STORM, ANTI-SHARED-DATABASE-COUPLING, ANTI-UNBOUNDED-QUEUE: public/design-spec.js:253-278
- Chapter 9 link: threats and controls target contracts and data (public/security-domain.js:25). Warnings: unassessed (:133), RESILIENCE-MISSING for external contracts (:138), unclassified data (:139), personal data with no disclosure threat (:143)
- Chapter 10 link: one runtime path is seeded per environment and contract (public/runtime-domain.js:74-99), with path policy and crossing-control checks (:259-261). The execution agreement requires the contract's runtime path in its named environment: public/architecture-applicability.js:73
- Chapter 5 link: components own, consume and produce Chapter 8 definitions, and two components claiming one record is an error: public/realisation-domain.js:158,236
- Chapter 4 link: the impact preview names the Chapter 4 responsibilities that affected components realise (public/interface-impact.js:42). Chapter 4 has no information model of its own (public/record-fields.js:136)
- SDD §4: per-component Interfaces, Data model (owned, consumed and produced definitions with keys), Invariants and Lifecycles (public/sdd-architecture.js:991-1003); External integrations, invariants, batch chain and execution agreements (:849-856). The Chapter 8 register lists contracts, parties, data dictionary, lineage, invariants and checks: public/interfaces-domain.js:406-409
- Sol in Chapter 8 proposes only: field drafts never cover owners, identifiers, evidence or numbers (intelligence-service.js:338,344). The Chapter 8 rule-based Co-design proposals need reviewed:true (public/interfaces-domain.js:365-366,395).

**Gaps.**

- **[high | 5 Application realization / 8 Interfaces & data | effort S] Two ownership records disagree with no finding (Chapter 5 dataIds vs Chapter 8 authority)**
  - AIW: Ownership is held twice: component dataIds (public/realisation-domain.js:158) and Chapter 8 authorityId/authorities (public/interfaces-domain.js:191-197). The authority is copied from the owning component only when the definition is seeded (:134). Probe on bank-payment: I set DAT-001's authority to risk-engine while APP-001 (api-pod) still owns it in Chapter 5. No Chapter 5 or Chapter 8 ownership finding appeared; only the existing classification, retention, review and assumption warnings remained. The architecture-edition SDD printed 'owns DAT-001…; consumes DAT-001…; produces DAT-001' for APP-001 without remark.
  - Handbook: Data_Architecture_Handbook › The boundaries every data design must name ('Authority boundary: who may create or correct it?'); › Data Ownership and Stewardship (DAT-GV-001); › Shared Mutable Data without Accountable Ownership (DAT-AP-001). AGENTS.md: 'Preserve one canonical Design Graph'.
  - Recommendation: Make Chapter 8 authorities (role 'owns') the one source and derive Chapter 5 'owns' from it, migrating the old path rather than keeping both (AGENTS.md engineering doctrine). Until then, raise a rule-based cross-chapter error when they disagree. A person resolves it; Sol may only explain.
  - Skeptic: HOLDS — The main claim holds, but the SDD evidence and the recommendation need correcting. I re-ran the probe (bank-payment through the API, port 4783, scratch DB under /home/user/hb-review/scratch/skeptic-data/, stopped by PID). After setting DAT-001's authority to risk-engine, finalFindings showed no new finding at all. The canonical graph then held two conflicting edges, 'api-pod owns instruction via realisation.components.dataIds' and 'risk-engine owns instruction via interfaces.data.authorityId' (public/architecture-model.js:175,199), and model.issues was empty. The SDD did NOT print 'APP-001 owns DAT-001' after the change. It printed APP-002 as the owner, because component ownership is already derived from Chapter 8 authorities first, and component dataIds count only where no authority exists (public/design-spec.js:152-158). The analyst's quote came from the unchanged project: their probe4.mjs exports bp.json without the change. So the 'derive Chapter 5 owns from Chapter 8' recommendation is already done for the component spec and the SDD. What remains is retiring Chapter 5 dataIds as a second ownership record in the graph, the Chapter 5 checks (public/realisation-domain.js:158) and the impact preview (public/interface-impact.js:44), or raising a finding when the two disagree. Also, in a blank project dataIds may only name Chapter 4 data nodes (public/realisation-domain.js:234), so the conflict mainly arises in the reference project and in task-created data.

## evolution-and-transition

**Question.** Does AIW support EVOLUTION and TRANSITION architecture as the handbooks prescribe? This covers capturing the current-state baseline, the target, transition states (plateaus), gap analysis, migration patterns, the roadmap and its work packages, dependencies, technical debt, decommissioning, fitness functions and drift, and versioned baselines under change control.

**Answer.** Partly. Versioned baselines and change control are AIW's strongest area here. Baselines are frozen with SHA-256 per part, the roadmap and change journal are included, any two states can be compared, and governance needs a second signed-in person. Live run: BL-001 froze with its plateaus and MIG-001 and verified true. The transition model in Chapter 11 works but is thin: current, transition and target plateaus; Chapter 4–10 records marked as introduced or retired per plateau; migration records whose reconciliation, parallel-run, rollback and rehearsal fields are free text; warning-level plan checks; an SDD Transition section and gap plan; and ArchiMate export. Several items are only partial or reachable only through Sol or the API. Brownfield intake has requirement delivery statuses and a 'legacy system'/'retiring' marker on external parties. Imported models carry no current-state meaning, and ArchiMate Plateau, WorkPackage and Gap elements are dropped or mis-mapped; verified, a Gap became a drafted Chapter 1 requirement. Migration records and group placements have no editor, so they arrive only as drafter (Sol) proposals or raw API commands. Chapter 7 offers a migration/exit procedure as text. Fitness checking is typed verification checks on accepted obligations, compared with imported observations, and drift is only an obligation template. AIW cannot record: an as-is inventory or register of recovered claims; gates, invariants or operability per plateau; dependency checks across plateaus (verified: three contracts still point at endpoints retired by the target, with no finding); dependencies between actions or a critical path; components marked temporary with an expiry; decommissioning readiness; a technical-debt register; a migration strategy or pattern choice; outcome links on the roadmap; or continuous fitness functions and drift against the deployed system.

**Skeptic on the answer.** The direct answer is broadly right but omits or overstates a few points. (1) It implies there are no proposal branches. AIW has named, saved model alternatives through alternative.save, alternative.apply, alternative.archive and alternative.rollback (public/model-alternatives.js:18-20, :58-64; public/alternative-editor.js:26), limited to 1-12 existing Chapter 4-7 records. They branch from the working design, not from a baseline, and exclude the roadmap. (2) It misses the core-banking domain lens's 'Migration and coexistence' concern (public/lenses/core-banking.js:337-357). That concern gives a word-level, item-to-confirm reading of migration plan, reconciliation, coexistence or cut-over (a plateau counts), rehearsal and decommissioning, so these are partly supported for core-banking projects only. (3) It understates the migration gap: a person cannot keep or dismiss a drafted migration record at all. record.keep and record.retire do not cover roadmap collections (public/record-lifecycle.js:50), so the record stays 'proposed' unless a raw review.migration or review.migration-remove command is sent. (4) Change control does detect roadmap changes. They bump finalReview.version and make the baseline non-current (public/review-domain.js:380, :434, :50, :251). What is missing is only the record-level diff. (5) Minor points. A baseline is stored as six parts (source, review, journal, findings, model, markdown; project-storage.js:118), built in five freeze steps. The ArchiMate export code is public/model-exchange.js:73-92; roadmap-validate.mjs:153-179 is its test. Delivery actions can name Chapter 1 outcomes as linked objects (public/review-domain.js:124; public/review-ui.js:108), so 'no outcome links on the roadmap' holds for plateaus but not strictly for actions.

**What AIW does today.**

- (Root: /home/user/AIW-Brain/AIW-V5-Local-Source.) Plateaus of kind current, transition or target, with a unique order, an optional due date and a purpose. A design has one current state and up to 50 plateaus, and every edit is versioned with who and when — public/roadmap-domain.js:18-20, public/roadmap-domain.js:88-104 (live: a second 'current' plateau was refused)
- Any Chapter 4–10 record can be marked as introduced in one plateau and retired in a later one, and the order is enforced. Chapter 1–3 records are treated as motivation that holds at every plateau, and assigning REQ-001 was refused live — public/roadmap-domain.js:55-60, public/roadmap-domain.js:117-127
- Reading the design as of a plateau, marks on the model pictures (introduced, not yet introduced, retired) and a 'By plateau' build-up — public/roadmap-domain.js:183-218, public/roadmap-ui.js:90
- Readiness per plateau, counted from the chapters' own checks and the states of the actions; it approves nothing — public/roadmap-domain.js:226-247
- Plan checks raised as Chapter 11 warnings: ROADMAP-NO-ACTION, -NO-PATH, -ORDER, -UNPLANNED, -MIGRATION (a legacy or retiring party, or migration words, with no plan) and -MIGRATION-LINKS — public/roadmap-domain.js:264-293, public/review-domain.js:290-292
- Migration record (MIG-nnn, at most 20) with source, target, scope, volumes, reconciliation, parallel run, rollback and rehearsal, each stated or left open, tied to plateaus and delivery actions — public/roadmap-domain.js:27-29, public/roadmap-domain.js:140-159
- A delivery action (RA-nnn) can name the plateau it works towards, with owner, due date and acceptance — public/review-domain.js:325, public/roadmap-domain.js:172-178
- SDD Transition section: a plateau table plus one gap per step (records introduced and retired, actions with their states), stating 'Nothing here establishes that a transition has happened' — public/roadmap-domain.js:300-321
- SDD §6 refinements: Versions, Since BL-nnn, change summary, Plateaus, Gap plan to the next version, Migration and Retired records — public/sdd-architecture.js:1515-1545, public/sdd-architecture.js:1562-1615
- ArchiMate export writes each plateau as Plateau, each step as Gap and each action as WorkPackage, with triggerings — roadmap-validate.mjs:153-179 (suite run: 11 checks pass)
- Brownfield requirements workbook with delivery, validation and reliability assertions, manual intervention and historical UAT counts — public/intake-fields.js:3-13. Checks on those assertions (delivery gaps, unstable delivered items, statuses that conflict with tests) — public/assurance-domain.js:51-80
- The drafter proposes 'Current platform' and 'Target platform' plateaus, places requirement groups by their delivery status, adds obligations to close value gaps and a migration record with open fields. These arrive as sol-draft proposals for a person to accept — public/architecture-drafter.js:1005-1029, roadmap-validate.mjs:208-213
- External party kind 'legacy system' and status current, target or retiring — public/interfaces-domain.js:26-27, public/interfaces-domain.js:40
- Chapter 7 'Design migration or exit' for a technology realization: trigger, sequence, data plan, stop criteria, fallback, verification and decommission, with a dated source. It is saved as lifecyclePlan text plus a TRANS record marked 'Draft operating procedure; execution evidence required' — public/workbench-domain.js:77-82, public/workbench-domain.js:196, public/workbench-ui.js:105. Cost comparison over a horizon including migration and exit costs — public/architecture-analysis.js:53
- Baseline freeze runs as a job. It captures five parts (design source; review state with the change journal and roadmap; findings; connected model; frozen SDD), each with a SHA-256, plus parentId lineage, and has open and verify endpoints — public/review-domain.js:539-561, baseline-service.js:1-22, baseline-service.js:139-169 (live: BL-001's review part contained PLT-001..003 and MIG-001, and verify returned true)
- Baseline comparison: any two baselines, or a baseline against the working design, record by record and edge by edge — public/workbench-actions.js:33, public/architecture-analysis.js:59-63. Changes since the last baseline — public/sdd-document.js:66-77. Chapter-level changes and whether a baseline is still current — public/review-domain.js:251, public/review-domain.js:333
- Governance evidence per baseline needs a second signed-in person (never an agent). The handoff needs a current, approved, architect-reviewed baseline. 'Ready for governance' is refused while change reviews are open — public/review-domain.js:425-428
- Change journal: every change with fields before and after, the records it affects and their reviews, published as an SDD annex in Markdown, CSV or JSON — change-journal.js:1-40, public/changes-domain.js:1-12
- Model reconciliation: a new revision of an imported model file is compared with AIW through reviewed identity links, field by field across three versions, and the person explicitly keeps the current value or takes the incoming one — public/model-reconciliation.js:1-2, public/model-reconciliation.js:46-93 (validation suite PASS)
- Typed verification checks (metric or assertion, operator, environment, release) on actions tied to accepted obligations, compared with observations imported through aiw.delivery.v1 as pass, fail, unknown or stale — public/architecture-verification.js:1-21, public/delivery-feedback.js:8-32
- Drift obligation template 'Check implementation against the baseline' and the co-design idea 'Conformance to the baseline' — public/review-domain.js:330, public/review-ui.js:156
- Read-only what-if branch for changed drivers that re-reads the affected decisions — public/architecture-whatif.js:1-6. Reversal of an applied design alternative — public/model-reversal.js:1-20
- Sol and agent guard: drafts are proposals; review.baseline, governance, intake, disposition, record, handoff and delivery are person-only. review.plateau, review.migration and review.action are not on that list, so drafts of them arrive in the proposed state — authorship.js:37-39, authorship.js:55-60
- Runtime plan rollout strategy and rollback/compatibility text fields; contract version and versioning policy — public/record-fields.js:192, public/record-fields.js:165

**Gaps.**

- **[high | 11 Review & realize (Plateaus) | effort S] Migration records and group placements have no UI editor, and the SDD points to a stage that does not exist**
  - AIW: The Plateaus stage offers only Add, Edit and Remove for plateaus and record assignment (public/roadmap-ui.js:1-8, :72-87). The Chapter 11 stages are overview, case, document, delivery and plateaus (public/review-ui.js:130). The migration fields (parallelRun and the rest) appear only in roadmap-domain.js, architecture-drafter.js and sdd-architecture.js; Chapter 11 has no draft editor registered (public/studio-kit.js:118). The SDD tells readers to use 'Chapter 11 · Work · Delivery plan' (public/sdd-architecture.js:1567, :1586). So a migration record reaches a person only as a drafter (Sol) proposal (public/architecture-drafter.js:1023-1029) or through the raw commands API; I created MIG-001 that way in the live run.
  - Handbook: Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › EVO-GV-001 Outcome anchored migration roadmap › Implementation approach ('Represent the roadmap as linked records: outcome, measure, hypothesis, capability, transition state, work package, evidence gate, and owner'). Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › EVO-FND-10 Migration units determine feasible sequencing › Required outputs.
  - Recommendation: Add a person-authored Migration editor and a group-placement control to the Plateaus stage, sending review.migration and review.plateau-group with open-field states, and let a person keep, edit or dismiss drafted migration proposals there. Point the SDD at the real stage. Proposals stay proposed until a person saves them.
  - Skeptic: HOLDS — Holds, and the gap is wider than stated. Forms exist only for review.plateau and review.plateau-assign (public/record-fields.js:79, :214-215). There is no form for review.migration or review.plateau-group, and public/roadmap-ui.js:72-87 routes only plateau-add, -edit, -remove and -assign. The citation of public/studio-kit.js:118 is beside the point: that registry holds canvas draft editors, not forms. A person also cannot keep or dismiss a drafted MIG record. The drafter marks it state 'proposed' (public/architecture-drafter.js:1281), and record.keep and record.retire find records only through COLLECTIONS, which has no roadmap.* entry (public/record-lifecycle.js:50, :65-68). The record therefore stays a proposal until someone sends a raw review.migration or review.migration-remove command (public/roadmap-domain.js:140-166). The SDD's pointer 'Chapter 11 · Work · Delivery plan' (public/sdd-architecture.js:1567, :1586) does not match any stage name. The nearest stage is 'Delivery actions' (public/review-ui.js:130), and it edits actions only. Group placements are printed in the SDD (public/sdd-architecture.js:1546-1556), but the Plateaus stage never shows them: public/roadmap-ui.js:13 does not import plateauGroups.
- **[high | 1 Requirements / X6 Intake and import | effort M] ArchiMate import drops plateaus and work packages and turns a Gap into a Chapter 1 requirement**
  - AIW: The interpreter maps WorkPackage to task, Plateau to boundary and Gap to requirement, and marks them only with layer 'implementation' (public/model-interpreter.js:439-444, :455). That marker is read only to keep work packages out of journey steps (public/journey-drafter.js:534). Every element of kind 'requirement' becomes a drafted Chapter 1 requirement (public/journey-drafter.js:607). Live run: a model with Plateaus 'Baseline 2026' and 'Target 2028', the Gap 'Replace posting engine' and a WorkPackage produced no plateaus. The Gap was drafted as a requirement, then as Chapter 2 driver 'Quality: Replace posting engine' and as a Chapter 3 decision. The legacy and new components were drafted side by side with no current/target distinction. Export does write Plateau, Gap and WorkPackage (roadmap-validate.mjs:153-179), so the round trip is lossy.
  - Handbook: Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › EVO-FND-01 Architecture includes the path of change › Required outputs ('Current target and intermediate views'). Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › EVO-DI-001 Architecture recovery and dependency mapping › How the mechanism works. Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › EVO-DI-006 Inventory treated as complete architectural truth.
  - Recommendation: Map imported Plateau elements to review.plateau proposals, plateau Composition to plateau-assign proposals, WorkPackage to review.action proposals carrying a plateauId, and Gap to the gap narrative, never to a requirement. Mark elements composed into the baseline plateau as current state. Everything stays proposed, cites its passages and waits for a person to accept it; reconciliation keeps working through identity bindings.
  - Skeptic: HOLDS — Confirmed. Plateau is mapped to 'boundary', WorkPackage to 'task' and Gap to 'requirement', all marked layer 'implementation' (public/model-interpreter.js:439, :444, :455). Work packages are kept out of journey steps (public/journey-drafter.js:534), and every element of kind 'requirement' becomes a drafted Chapter 1 requirement (public/journey-drafter.js:607). The export lives in public/model-exchange.js:73-92; roadmap-validate.mjs:153-179 only tests it. Even AIW's own export does not round-trip: it writes aiw-id, aiw-type and aiw-attributes properties (public/model-exchange.js:82), and the interpreter never reads them (model-interpreter.js contains no 'aiw-type').
- **[high | 1 Requirements and 4–10 (brownfield intake) | effort L] No current-state (as-is) inventory and no register of recovered-architecture claims**
  - AIW: The map lists 'current-state (as-is) landscape, existing system inventory' as record types not available (aiw-map part-1 §1.9 item 5). Brownfield state can be expressed only by assigning a record to the 'current' plateau (public/roadmap-domain.js:117-127), by a party's 'legacy system'/'retiring' status (public/interfaces-domain.js:40), or by requirement delivery assertions (public/intake-fields.js:3-13). A component's status is draft, candidate or reviewed only, never existing, modified or to-retire (public/record-fields.js:144). Imported elements carry only stated or inferred confidence (public/model-interpreter.js:18-19). No CMDB or IaC import exists (aiw-map part-3 X6). 'Review inherited project' means the upstream chapters, not a legacy estate (public/review-domain.js:382).
  - Handbook: Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › EVO-DI-001 Architecture recovery and dependency mapping › Implementation approach ('Store the recovery model as reviewable structured claims… A claim can be supported, disputed, superseded or retired'). Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › EVO-DI-003 Characterization testing and behavioural baselines. Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › EVO-DI-006 Inventory treated as complete architectural truth.
  - Recommendation: Add a disposition to each Chapter 4–10 record (exists today, new, modified, to retire), defaulted from the current-plateau assignment. Add a recovered-claim register built on the existing evidence-source machinery (SHA-256, passages) with corroborated, disputed and superseded states, and keep contradictions visible. Recovered claims are project evidence, not released knowledge, and never feed scoring.
  - Skeptic: HOLDS — Confirmed (aiw-map part-1 §1.9 item 5; public/record-fields.js:144; public/interfaces-domain.js:40; public/model-interpreter.js:15-19). One reading the analyst missed: for core-banking projects that choose the lens, its 'Migration and coexistence' concern checks whether any record's words mention a current or legacy platform (public/lenses/core-banking.js:337-357). That is a word reading that blocks nothing (public/domain-lens.js:1-12), not an inventory or a claim register.
- **[high | 11 Review & realize (Plateaus) | effort L] Plateaus carry no gate predicates, invariants, authority, operability or per-transition decision record**
  - AIW: A plateau holds only id, revision, title, kind, order, purpose and due (public/roadmap-domain.js:100). The plan checks test only whether actions are linked, orders hold and a path exists (public/roadmap-domain.js:276-293). Readiness counts the passages of the chapters' own checks (public/roadmap-domain.js:226-247). Drivers and decisions are 'motivation' that holds at every plateau (public/roadmap-domain.js:55-60). Governance is one record per whole-design baseline, not per transition (public/review-domain.js:427), and its conditions are free text (aiw-map part-3 §11.9).
  - Handbook: Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › EVO-GV-002 Transition decision records and stage gates › How the mechanism works (gate predicates with named evidence, reviewer, freshness and failure response; bind evidence to revisions). Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › EVO-GV-001 Outcome anchored migration roadmap › How the mechanism works ('Define transition states that are independently operable… writer authority, contract compatibility, accepted-work ownership, and recovery options'). Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › Decision record template (Current and intermediate states; Invariants and obligations; Failure and recovery).

Add a person-only transition decision command (four eyes, never Sol or an agent) bound to a baseline stamp. Unknown never counts as pass.

Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › EVO-RT-002 Dependency drain and consumer retirement. Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › EVO-FND-10 Migration units determine feasible sequencing › Required outputs ('Dependency graph').

  - AIW: A retiredIn assignment carries no exit predicates, responsibility inventory, archive access, credential revocation or contract closure (public/roadmap-domain.js:117-127). The only exit support is the Chapter 7 'Design migration or exit' procedure, which is text (decommission: 'Retirement, retention and disposal') saved as lifecyclePlan plus a TRANS record marked 'Draft operating procedure' and not linked to any plateau (public/workbench-domain.js:77-82, :196; public/workbench-ui.js:105).   - Recommendation: When a record gets a retiredIn plateau, open a decommission record: each responsibility with a disposition (transferred, completed, retained or no longer required), exit predicates bound to evidence, archive and retention taken from Chapter 8 retentionDays, credential revocation from Chapter 9 controls, and contract closure. Neither records responsibilities, exit predicates or revocation. Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › Decision record template › Expiry and exit.

- **[medium | 11 Review & realize (cross-cutting) | effort M] No technical-debt register**
  - AIW: Outside catalogue prose (public/playbook-knowledge.js, public/knowledge-pack.js) the source never mentions debt. Debt can be expressed only as an 'Accepted design limitation' disposition, which has no owner field, impact, remediation plateau or expiry (public/review-domain.js:71-72; aiw-map part-3 X2: 'Accepted limitations have no expiry or re-review date').

Record that distinction and decide whether the migration must remediate it before expansion'). Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › EVO-ST-001 Strangler Fig modernisation › Failure diagnosis ('Temporary architecture becomes permanent debt').

Show them in the SDD and the Plateaus stage. Only a person creates or closes one; Sol may propose.

A finding can also be treated as 'Carry as action', which links a current delivery action with an owner, due date, acceptance and plateauId (public/review-domain.js:415, :325). An 'Accepted design limitation' records a reviewer, a rationale with the remaining exposure, and a reference (public/record-fields.js:209). Debt arising outside findings, and impact, review date or expiry, still cannot be recorded. 'Debt' also appears in catalogue prose in public/brain-catalogue.js:353, which the analyst did not list.

  - Handbook: Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › EVO-VA-005 Governed AI assisted modernisation ('The model has no autonomous architectural or business authority… authorised humans decide'). Evolution_Architecture_Volume_8_Handbook › Decision record template › Decision and authority ('Keep a model suggestion distinct from an accountable decision').
  - Recommendation: Mark proposed or Sol-drafted plateaus in the Transition table and the Plateaus stage ('drafted, not reviewed'), as migration records already are. Keep them out of readiness counts until a person saves them, matching the record-lifecycle rule for proposals.
  - Skeptic: HOLDS — Holds for readiness, findings and the Transition and Plateaus tables (public/roadmap-domain.js:51, :226-321; public/sdd-architecture.js:1546-1556). The SDD does mark them elsewhere, though. Appendix B lists every Sol-drafted plateau and migration record still in the proposed state as 'Drafted by Sol, to keep, change or retire' (public/sdd-architecture.js:139, :1659-1661). A person clears a drafted plateau's proposed state by saving it through review.plateau (public/roadmap-domain.js:99-100), not through record.keep, which does not reach roadmap records (public/record-lifecycle.js:50).

## security-architecture

**Question.** Does AIW support SECURITY ARCHITECTURE as the handbooks prescribe?

**Answer.** Partly. Chapter 9 covers the handbooks' core chain from threat to control to evidence to residual risk. A threat names an actor, scenario, consequence, owner and the Chapter 5–8 objects it affects, including Chapter 6 trust boundaries. A control records its enforcement point, mechanism, failure posture, telemetry and verification plan. Evidence goes stale when its control changes, the architect's risk acceptance is kept apart from governance evidence, and Chapter 10 must bind every control to a runtime plan. AIW CANNOT pick controls from a control catalogue or security patterns: Chapter 9 has no picker, and 25 of the catalogue's 26 security patterns are template text that a person reaches only through Sol, the knowledge workspace or the Chapter 3 alternative datalist. It also cannot model identity and access or the lifecycle of keys and secrets, record which flows are allowed and which must be denied, or map obligations to controls and evidence (a Chapter 1 rule-to-control link is stored, but nothing reads it). Nor does it require that a different person approve a residual risk the architect accepted: I recorded an acceptance and its own 'approved' governance record in one step, and the open finding cleared. Threat elicitation, encryption, logging, privacy and security testing are only PARTLY supported, through free-text fields and prompts that block nothing. The handbooks never mention STRIDE, likelihood-and-impact scoring or PCI DSS (none of the three appears in any of the 29), so their absence from AIW is not a handbook gap. What the handbooks do ask for is a six-step threat-modelling method (assets, trust boundaries, misuse scenarios, controls, validation, residual risk) using spoofing, tampering, disclosure, denial-of-service and privilege-misuse questions, plus an obligation applicability register and evidence organised by claim.

**Skeptic on the answer.** The overall verdict, 'Partly', is right. Several statements in the direct answer need fixing.

(1) 'It also cannot model identity and access' should read 'only partly, as free text'. A Chapter 7 realization requires 'Identity and access obligations', with an error-level finding when empty (public/record-fields.js:154; public/technology-realisation-domain.js:208). A Chapter 10 runtime path requires 'Network admission and identity enforcement' (record-fields.js:200; runtime-domain.js:259). Chapter 8 requires authorization text (interfaces-domain.js:302). What is missing is a structured model.

(2) 'Cannot record which flows are allowed and which must be denied' is overstated. Chapter 10 runtime paths are per-contract permitted-flow records with typed boundaryIds (record-fields.js:200). Denials can be written only as prose in the boundary crossing policy, whose help text asks 'what is refused' (record-fields.js:247). What is missing is typed denied flows and negative counterparts.

(3) 'A Chapter 1 rule-to-control link is stored, but nothing reads it': the Chapter 1 register export prints the link (requirements-domain.js:305). Released regulation records are also shown on Chapter 1 constraints and Chapter 2 compliance drivers (knowledge-ui.js:71-76). No check, Chapter 9 view or SDD section reads either.

(4) 'Recorded an acceptance and its own approved governance record in one step': it took two separate commands, security.risk and then security.governance, by the same actor. I reproduced this on a live server, where risk-approval cleared after the approval.

(5) The '25 of 26 security patterns ... reached only through Sol, the knowledge workspace or the Chapter 3 datalist' wording is fine for catalogue patterns. But AIW also offers playbook security patterns (P-PROTECTED-SYSTEM, P-SECURE-COMMUNICATION) through a Chapter 5 component pattern picker (realisation-ui.js:76) and a drafted Chapter 3 'identity' question (architecture-drafter.js:579). Chapter 9 also has five deterministic control templates (security-domain.js:161-165).

(6) The handbook set has 27 files, not 29 (/home/user/hb-review/*.md; chunks/index.json lists 27 handbooks). It is still true that none mentions STRIDE or PCI DSS, and none asks for likelihood × impact scoring. The QA_Environment_Handbook mentions 'consequence, likelihood, detectability' only for allocating test effort.

(7) Two smaller points. Chapter 10 requires binding only for controls whose target assets fall in that environment's scope (runtime-domain.js:71, :222), not literally every control. The Pattern DNA protection question can also be answered by an identity need or an identity realization, not only by a Chapter 9 control (pattern-dna.js:198).

The '17 security patterns' set was not found. The likely source is the 17 candidate patterns in candidate-sets/core-banking-candidate-pack.json. Only three of them touch security, and they are correctly never offered.

**What AIW does today.**

- Threat record (THR-nnn): title, actor, scenario, consequence, accountable risk owner, priority Critical/High/Moderate/Low with a priority basis and who set it, assumptions, requirement grounds; the domain refuses a threat with no affected object (public/security-domain.js:17, :79-87; public/record-fields.js:179)
- Affected and protected objects span Chapter 8 contracts and data, Chapter 5 components, Chapter 7 realizations, Chapter 6 capabilities and Chapter 6 trust boundaries; requirements, drivers and decisions are inherited through those targets (public/security-domain.js:25, :42-44)
- Six threat categories that roughly cover the handbook's STRIDE-like concerns: Unauthorised action, Replay or tampering, Data disclosure, Missing evidence, Service disruption, Dual control & segregation of duties (public/security-domain.js:14-16)
- Control record (SEC-nnn): purpose, owner, enforcement point, mechanism, failure posture (Deny or contain / Detect and respond / Explicit exception) with a failure response, telemetry, verification plan, operating obligations; 8 categories including Identity & access, Data protection, Secrets & keys, Detection & audit; up to 50 parameter rows such as a rotation period; maker and checker roles for a dual control (public/security-domain.js:15, :88-99; public/record-fields.js:180-183, :289)
- Chapter 9 checks: control-definition, control-links, control-rationale (no threat), control-scope, failure, assurance-plan, source change, dual-control-roles/same/uncovered, threat-definition, uncovered (an affected object with no control and no accept/avoid treatment), unassessed Chapter 8 contracts/data, classification, personal-data, RESILIENCE-MISSING, risk-review, risk-approval, risk-due, simulation (public/security-domain.js:104-149)
- Evidence of three kinds (design, implementation, verification) with result pass/partial/fail, reviewer, reference and observations. It is stamped against the current control, so it goes stale when the control or its inputs change; withdrawn evidence stays in history (public/security-domain.js:18, :46-51, :175-176)
- Assurance labels per control ('Planned · evidence needed', 'Design review recorded', 'Implementation evidence recorded', 'Test pass recorded', ...) close to the BPR T03 status vocabulary; the UI says evidence is user-recorded and not verified by AIW (public/security-domain.js:51; public/security-ui.js:85)
- Residual risk per threat: treatment mitigate/accept/avoid, residual, owner, a valid next-review date, reviewer, reference, rationale, stamped against threat + controls + evidence. A separate governance record (approved/rejected/deferred) is refused until a current treatment exists. 'risk-approval' stays a warning until a governance record says 'approved' (public/security-domain.js:125-129, :177-178; public/record-fields.js:185-186)
- Threat walkthrough in 'planned' or 'control unavailable' mode, four stages that must all be visited before the observations are recorded (public/security-domain.js:166-168, :179)
- Fingerprint traceability: each threat and control keeps a reading of its targets and named requirements; a change marks only those records 'source' stale (public/security-domain.js:32-39, :113, :122)
- Guided security design task in four steps (exposure, enforcement, failure response, testable claim) that prepares a reviewable alternative; accepting it needs a reviewer and a reason, and creates no evidence, risk treatment or governance record (public/security-design.js:7-12, :56; security-design-validate.mjs:23-27; security-task-ui-validate.mjs:53-54)
- Deterministic, non-LLM control templates (authority, replay, minimise, audit, resilience) and journey-drafter threats: Data disclosure per Confidential/Restricted data, Replay or tampering per external contract, Service disruption per external contract and per zone (public/security-domain.js:160-165, :197-238; public/journey-drafter.js:419-432)
- Sol and agent accounts cannot send a payload that claims reviewed, confirmed, approved or acknowledged, so they cannot record evidence, risk or governance (authorship.js:37-63)
- Upstream protection inputs: Chapter 8 contracts need authorization and transport (error 'protection'); data has 6 classification levels, a check for fields more sensitive than their definition, personal-data markers, free-text residency and free-text protection ('access, masking and storage') (public/interfaces-domain.js:21-22, :188, :302, :330-331, :339)
- Chapter 6 trust boundaries need a title, owner and crossing policy. A person's project starts with three proposed boundaries (Channel entry, Application services, Restricted state). Capabilities must name a boundary; identity and transactional capabilities 'must not fail open' (public/technology-domain.js:187, :242, :357-361)
- A Chapter 1 Rule records authority, instrument, section and effective date, and can name 'the control that enforces it (Chapter 9)'; statement checks recognise PCI DSS, GDPR, DORA, PSD2 and others (public/requirements-domain.js:177-178, :212; public/statement-checks.js:43)
- The Pattern DNA 'protection' question opens on Chapter 5/6 records with security, privacy or compliance attributes or exposed parts, and is answered only by a Chapter 9 control on the target (public/pattern-dna.js:191-203; public/design-questions.js:118; public/realisation-domain.js:168)
- Chapter 10 binding: every control whose targets touch an environment's assets must be bound to a runtime plan (error unbound-SEC-nnn); a bound control needs an enforcement owner; a warning when it has no current passing verification; a warning when a path crosses a trust boundary and its access text names no control of that boundary; a hosting zone must hold a trust boundary (public/runtime-domain.js:71, :222, :250-251, :256-261)
- Model: a 'Threat model' view of parties, components, stores and trust regions, with each crossing marked guarded, exposed or unexamined, and a threats × controls matrix. Its insights (unexamined crossings, threats not fully covered, confidential data leaving the system edge with no control, components outside any boundary, controls with no evidence) appear as prompts in Validate's Model integrity lens (public/threat-model.js:1-7, :231-263; public/validation-models.js:26, :40-44; threat-validate.mjs:35-70, :210)
- Output: Chapter 9 Security register (Markdown/HTML); SDD §4 per-component 'Protection:' line; §5 'Security & trust' figure; reviewers' 'Assurance' part ('A planned control is a design intent until evidence shows it works'); executive edition 'What protects it'; Chapter 11 trail step 5 'Protection and runtime' (public/security-domain.js:187-188; public/sdd-architecture.js:1024-1025, :1755; public/sdd-document.js:378-381)
- Run locally (scratch under /home/user/hb-review/scratch/security-arch). Bank Payment reference: 4 threats and 3 controls; every control fails control-definition, failure and assurance-plan; 3 threats are 'uncovered'; Chapter 10 raises unbound-SEC-001..003. Clinic appointments: 8 threats (6 Data disclosure, 1 Replay, 1 Service disruption), each with a threat-definition error because actor and owner are open; the SDD has no security section (sdd-bank.md headings run 1–7 with only §5 'Chapter 9 · Security & trust')

**Gaps.**

The record stores no actor (fields: id, threatId, stamp, at, decision, reviewer, reference, observations), and four eyes in provenance applies only to confirm/keep (public/provenance.js:396-398). review.governance does require a different signed-in, non-agent actor (public/review-domain.js:266-274). An 'accept' treatment clears the 'uncovered' error at once (public/security-domain.js:125); missing approval is only the 'risk-approval' warning (:127); a past next-review date is only a warning (:129); SDD readiness counts only untreated errors (public/readiness-model.js:54, :74). Checked: the same session recorded 'accept', then an 'approved' governance record, and risk-approval cleared (/home/user/hb-review/scratch/security-arch/bin/gov.mjs). There is no delegated-authority level, no compensating-control field and no expiry action.
